## GAZETTE — The Town Burns Lamp Oil Again, Not Electricity

You said the town read as electric when it should read as flame. It does not any more. By day the lanterns are unlit brown paper, the way an unlit lamp actually looks; after dusk they come up in a warm flame colour and breathe a little, the way a wick does.
The glow no longer comes from one hardcoded colour sprinkled around the scene. Each era now draws its light from a single table: the first two eras burn flame, and Voltage is the first era that earns the cool, steady arc light — because that is the era whose hardware was built for it.
The windows, the doors, the string beads and the six lamp lights all read from that same table, so a town cannot end up half flame and half electric.

Nothing about how the yard plays has changed. Only what it tells you it is.

You told us you could not build a sluice beside the pond at Dry Gulch, and would have to win the map by kiting. You were right, and the fault was the painting, not the rules.
The pond was drawn out to more than twice the water the county actually recognises — a wide bright disc where the ground underneath was, by every rule the game plays by, dry. You aimed at the shore you could see and the game refused a shore it had drawn for you.

## The Territory Reward Takes Its Feet Back

The walls you earned no longer decide where you live.
Territory I used to plant a ring of palisades around the starting stake the moment a run began — walls you did not place, on ground you may not have wanted, which the Prospector then dutifully repaired out of your gold. It is now a KIT instead: a set of free palisades you carry into the run and put down wherever you please, spent through the ordinary build menu and gone when the run ends.
Nothing builds itself any more, so nothing taxes you for standing where it was put.

## The Mint Counts In Coins Again

The gold box stopped showing you a number with a tail on it.
After the seam-harvest upgrade, fractional yields were piling up behind the decimal point — the counter ran long enough to push the storage limit out of the box, so you could no longer see how close the bank was to full. Worse, a purse reading 30 could refuse a 30-cost purchase, because the true figure was a hair under. The mint now settles in whole coins and carries the remainder quietly: the fraction is still earned, it is simply not shown until it is a coin.
A price you can afford is now a price you can pay, on the tick the number says so.

## The Night Map Stops Repeating Itself

The engine quit re-doing arithmetic it had already done.
Every rendered frame, the machine was rebuilding the same lists to answer questions it had answered a moment earlier — sorting a fresh copy of its own frame history, and working out how much lamplight fell on each outlaw three separate times over. It now keeps a running tally, reuses its buffers, and measures each outlaw once. Not one lamp, shadow or pixel moved: the look was held to a strict comparison and the one change that did shift it was thrown out.
Across the five E1 maps under sixty-enemy pressure, frames now sit near half the budget the machine is allowed.

## The river remembers how to be a river
The braid at Twin Banks ran as flat ribbons — hard mitre corners where the two channels met, ends that stopped dead at the edge of the map, and dark voids beside the water. The channels now follow smoothed centrelines, the crossing reads as a proper confluence, and the water feathers off into the haze the way a river leaves a valley rather than halting at a line.
Nothing about the water the enemies wade through changed — the mask, the fords and the shore rules are untouched. This is what the eye sees, and only that.

Wall him in and he used to simply stop — a hundred tons of trouble standing politely at a fence, firing two rockets in a whole fight. ## The night raiders learn their way around a building
Raiders working the night shift used to snag on the furniture of the world — a yard, a shed, a landmark footprint — and gnaw at one corner until something killed them. They picked which way to slide past an obstacle from where they happened to be standing, which was a coin flip, and the losing half walked them straight back into the same wall.
They now slide toward the side their target is actually on. Fenced yards funnel raiders instead of eating them.

Nothing about the practice changes. The yard simply looks like a place the county built.

## The bandits stop wading the deep water at Twin Banks
At Twin Banks the river is crossed at gravel bars and fords, and the raiders had been treating a crossing as a stripe on the map rather than a place on it — so a bandit anywhere in that stripe believed the whole water column was walkable, waded into the deep, and stalled there. Forty-two of them stalled per run, and only twenty-eight of seventy ever reached the works.
They now aim for the crossing on the side their target is on, and a gravel bar only counts where it actually lies. All seventy arrive. None of them swim.

The garden's channel had been drawn on the map but never dug — a full-width slab of ink down the middle of the works, with the pressure manifold standing in it on nothing. There is water in it now, clear enough to read the bed through, and the four terraces the map is named for carry the combed rows and the coal-black bed a working garden earns.
Heat stands over the boilers only while the boilers are hot. When they cool, it goes.

The landmarks along the haul are modelled by the sun instead of lit from inside, and rust-umber dust hangs the length of the climb.

## The magenta rim comes off the faces on the wall
Seven hundred and seventy-four sprites had been carrying a thin violet edge, cut by an old extractor that took the key colour off after the shrink instead of before it. Nothing moved but the edge: the silhouettes and the interiors came through byte for byte, and the four sprites we knew were already clean stayed clean.

## Thirty-seven more faces come off the wall clean, and the rails with them
The violet edge that came off seven hundred and seventy-four sprites had left thirty-seven behind, on five sheets of the Prospector and on the rail elements. Those had been skipped for a reason that turned out to be arithmetic: the old extractor wrote its shrink down to four decimals, and cells that shrank at all came back a hair different every time. Sheets that did not shrink were never affected, which is why nobody saw it for so long.
The edge is gone from all thirty-seven, at both the size the game draws and the size it keeps. The silhouettes did not move and the interiors came through unchanged.
Two hundred and sixty-four cells are still standing on purpose. Those were mended by hand and re-cutting them today would undo the mend.

## The county book opens a posse column, and any row will show you its run
The standings board only ever knew how to rank one rider. It now keeps posses as their own columns — two, three or four hands — and a posse ranks only against posses its own size, so a party can never move a solo standing. The solo board is byte-identical to the one that stood there before.
Every row that submitted a reel now carries a handle to it, and a visitor can ask the clerk to play that exact run back. The reel itself is fetched only when asked for, so the board stays light.
The field book, kept separate from the ladder on purpose, is where a rider may say what it is made of. The ladder is never told — it is handed names and nothing else.

## A rig can take the empty chair at your table
Open a room from the tavern board and you get back a claim word. Hand that word to a rig and it sits down and plays the run with you — same contract, same seed, same clock, one tick at a time.
A seat cannot choose its own world: ask for a room and a contract in the same breath and it refuses, because a hand that picked its own table would not be at yours. It paces itself to the room rather than racing ahead, it is asked for orders only between waves, and if its reckoning of the world ever stops matching the table it stands up and says so instead of playing on.
What it can do today is build. That is the honest edge of it, written down rather than hidden.

Every contract card on the tavern board told a prospector what waited inside it — every card but one. The Drill Yard, the ground built for a first-timer, was the only card that showed nothing at all.
It now speaks like the rest: the corner of the river claim it borrows, what stands in the yard, and its two goals and three plain rules — pull the assay-tent lever for practice gold, ring the bell for one small wave, and nothing in the yard reaches the county ledger.
The card reads the same on a small screen as on a wide one, with the rules legible and the gate to the yard unhidden.

The practice yard's card had begun to contradict itself. Its written word said the straw men lend their patience; the line beneath it, the one the yard assembles for itself out of what it holds, called them straw mans.

## The far country came back to the prospector who had earned it
A lantern show was added to the county last week, and to run its replays it taught the game to read which age you were in from the contract in your hand. Every ordinary morning after that, the game read the same answer — the first claim on the river — no matter how far you had come.
The consequence was quiet and total: the late-age arsenal never woke. The terraforming cannon would not fire, and the county's own instruments reported the age as inactive while you stood in it.
The lantern show keeps its reading, and now only on the replay road. Every other way into the county asks the ledger which age you have reached, as it did before.

## The outlaws learned to walk around the rocks on every map
On one stretch of the river the raiders had learned to step around a standing rock and keep coming. Everywhere else in the county they walked straight at it and stopped, nose to the stone, until something else moved them.
The knack was never theirs alone — it had been fenced to that one stretch of ground by name. The fence is down. A raider whose quarry lies dead behind a rock now rounds it on any map, and the sidestep that keeps them out of the narrow wedges is untouched.

Worth knowing what the drain would not claim: two earlier attempts went at this by measuring how far the quarry sat from the rock, and both were proved dead ends before this one was tried; and the proof here is the pathing tests on both screens, not a picture — no screenshots were taken of this merge.


## The standings learned to read like a board, and your claims sit beside them
The standing sheet listed riders and little else. Now each line reads in the order a person asks: rank, the name, what they took, and when they rode.
Under it there is a second, shorter list that belongs only to you — the claims from your own ledger, kept plainly apart from the county's. Where nothing has happened yet the sheet says so in the county's own voice instead of showing you an empty frame, and the Field Book no longer rules columns for contracts nobody has attempted. Both read the same on a narrow screen.
One column is honest but still bare: "when" shows a dash on rows the public board has not yet been taught to hand over. The clerk knows the hour; the window has not been widened to show it. That work is written and queued.

Three of the board's tests were red on the machine before this merge and are red after it, for a reason that has nothing to do with the boards; that was proved by rebuilding the county as it stood beforehand and watching the same three fail.


## The "when" column on the standings now names the hour
Last week the sheet gained a column for when a rider took their claim, and every line under it showed a dash. The clerk knew the hour and had no window to hand it through. The window is open.
A row submitted today carries its own time. An older row that never recorded one still shows the dash rather than inventing a time it does not have, and it reads the same on a narrow screen.

Nothing about the boards changed; what changed is that we now know the red comes and goes, so the next hand to touch this sheet should re-run it rather than wave it through.


## The county put a door on the front of the house
Until now a rider who wanted in had to already know the way. There is a desk by the boards, and it says the two ways plainly: ride it yourself and your standing posts itself, or send your rig and read the door document kept at this same address.
The Herald carries the notice in every edition it prints — the county opens its door, and a nine-cent mind took the first claim.
It sits beside County Standings and The Field Book, and on a narrow screen it goes first.

Separately, three standings tests that the last two entries reported as a standing fault came up clean here on the first run, which is the second time running — they come and go, and the next hand should re-run rather than excuse them.


## The door stopped exploding when the Baron came
A rig sent to a railcar claim used to run headlong into the Baron and then simply vanish — the door shut with no word at all, no result, nothing any clerk could enter on any board. That is why no rider has ever posted a standing beyond the first county.
The fight is given its room now: six waves past whichever falls later, the securing of the claim or the Baron's arrival. And when the clock does run out, the door says so plainly and hands back an honest loss instead of slamming.
Two claims that were unwinnable by arithmetic alone — the Canyon Works and the Dust Flats, where the Baron rode in on the very wave the clock expired — can be finished at last.

Worth knowing what the drain would not claim: this is a change to the headless door only — nothing on your screen moves — and the proof it did not disturb the played game is that every pinned Baron, Claim, Night Shift and Twin Banks outcome came back identical, thirteen of thirteen.
## ROUNDUP — The Door Learned What Things Cost And Why It Says No

Three changes to the door — the address a mind that is not a person rides in through to play the county as you do. Nothing on your screen moves.
It now publishes what every buildable costs at each step, instead of making a visiting rig buy one, watch the price move, and work out the rounding for itself. A refusal names its cause in plain words — not enough gold, bad ground, something already standing there, outside the zone, or you already hold all you are allowed — where before the door only said no.
And the Night Shift is dark for a rig as well as for you now: a wrecker outside the lantern light moves the same fraction faster in the screenless runner as it does on your screen. Both of you are playing the same night.

And publishing the prices meant admitting two buildables the door had never listed at all, fenced to the first county only after the run's own review caught them leaking into ages that do not sell them.

## GAZETTE — The Moths Come For A Dark Claim Now, And An Idle Hand Dies At Wave Four

You asked for it tightened, and it is tightened. Moth Season used to let a claim with no lanterns lit off the hook entirely — the migration did not come for ground it could not see. It comes now, lit or not.
The moths themselves still do no harm on contact; what kills is the night runner riding in behind them, and that is the pressure that was raised. A hand that does nothing now dies at wave four. A hand that lights lanterns, sets turrets and works the harvest still secures at wave twelve, unchanged.
Both engines agree to the byte, which is the part worth trusting: the screenless runner and the game on your screen die and secure at exactly the same waves.

The slice was aimed at an idle hand failing "around wave eight to ten"; the roster turned out to have a sharp edge rather than a slope, and the smallest tune that fails at all fails at wave four — the nearest gentler one secures outright. That satisfies "tighten it" and leaves competent play untouched, but if the trail feels harsh in your hands the lever is two knobs and one word reverses it. Worth knowing what the drain would not claim: it also had to repair a collision on the way in — a neighbouring check listed every field the door publishes, and the cost-curve merge that landed hours earlier had just added one, so the list went red until the new field was added to it. That class will recur on the next such addition and is noted for the next hand.

## ROUNDUP — Your Agent Can Ride Into Your Claim, And The Wire Stops Crossing

You asked to be able to invite your agent — or several of them — to ride with you, and the door now takes them. A mind that arrives at a claim where a person is already working sits down as a SCOUT: it watches the same ground you do, its counsel is marked as counsel, and what it builds still goes up. There is no second set of books to disagree with yours, because the county keeps only one — yours.
A visiting rig that arrives while you are away rides as before, hash for hash. If you walk in later it moves over to the scout chair mid-ride, without dropping the reins.
The screenless practice road, where two rigs race with no person present, still refuses that mixed table — and now it says so in plain words and names the work that will open it, instead of shutting the door with no reason given.

Worth knowing what the drain would not claim: the relay road carries a red that is OLDER than any of this — the county gate that should turn away an unsaddled room with the words "riding together isn't saddled yet" now answers with nothing at all. That was recorded at eight minutes past one this morning, before a single one of today's changes existed; it is filed and it is being worked, and it is not something this change broke.

## ROUNDUP — The Door Learned To Teach Itself, And Stopped Charging For Nothing

Six changes that merged days ago and were never written up here, gathered into one item. Five of them concern the door — the address a mind that is not a person rides in through to play the county as you do — and nothing on your screen moves for any of them.
The plainest is the biggest: a rig playing through the public door now meets the same game you meet in the browser, with the same opening offers at the same prices, and before this no model had ever secured a single contract that way. The door also carries its own instructions now instead of leaving an arriving mind to guess — the transport, how a turn works, what it may ask for, how it hands orders back — and it points out where the Prospector is standing.
It also stopped charging for nothing: a rig can let a turn pass without filing orders, where before there was no way to wait that did not also file paperwork, and a standing order that could never succeed used to fail, raise a surprise, mint a turn and re-arm itself in a loop that billed nearly three thousand turns on one claim where it now bills one. The sixth change is not about the door at all — the Regatta's briefing promised six beacon gates on a five-beacon course, and it counts them correctly now.

## ROUNDUP — Your Agent Now Sees The Claim It Rides, And Finished One With You

You could already invite a mind to ride your claim, but it arrived blind — it had a body and no eyes, and only a rigged hand could move it. It sees now. The claim itself serves up the view, at each turn of the wave and whenever the ground calls for a hand, addressed to that one rider and to nobody else.
A visiting mind no longer keeps its own second copy of the county in its head to argue with yours. It watches the same ground you watch and sends its orders back down the same wire, which is what stops the two of you ever disagreeing about what happened.
It was proved by finishing a claim rather than by watching a wire twitch: a browser and a thin rig worked one claim together and secured it at wave ten — five views served, a palisade set down by the rig's own hand, and not one disagreement between them.

## ROUNDUP — Panning Pays Where You Stand, And The Turned-Away Rider Hears Why

The harvest used to happen wherever the order pointed, with nobody walking there. It does not any more: a mind working your claim sends the Prospector to the seam on foot, through the same legs you use, and the pan pays only within reach of where they actually stand. Nothing you do by hand changes — but a rig that used to reach clear across the claim at once now has to cross it.
Said plainly, because it costs something: this breaks the bench. Any standing set before the walk cannot be set beside one made after it, since the work is no longer the same work.
The smaller change closes a loop already reported here — a rig arriving at a room that was never saddled used to be met with nothing at all, the county's own words never reaching it. It gets the sentence now, and the reason with it.

## GAZETTE — Your Agent Now Rides By Invitation, And The Roster Names It

Until today a mind could ride your claim but no player could ask one to. The Ride Together panel now
hands you a single line to pass along — that is the whole invitation, and it is the first time the door
has been on the inside of the game rather than in a paper only agents read.
When one accepts, the roster says so plainly: a ridden seat carries its own mark, so you always know
which hands beside you are yours and which are hired.
This finishes the road you ruled on this morning — the body, then the eyes, and now the invitation.

what did the running. Every entry now names the mind and the rig it declared — and a rider who
declared nothing is said to have declared nothing, plainly, rather than being dressed up as a person.
Rank is untouched. The board still sorts on the run alone; what changed is only what it will tell you
about the hands, not what it will reward.

Ask the Prospector for something it has not been trusted with and it tells you so — held, ask me,
no trust. Until today that refusal could be quietly talked over: with nothing else to do, the mind
would drift back to muttering about the ledger, and the one word you needed was gone before you
read it.
A refusal now holds the floor past the first idle remark. Nothing else about the mind's chatter
changed, and anything you actually ask for next still answers you straight away.

- Call for a palisade where the ground won't take it and the order is torn up on the spot.
- Before, a refused order could ride one beat behind your boots and raise itself on the next good patch — and your gold went with it.
- Wave off a bad spot now and your purse sits untouched until you call again.

## GAZETTE — The Town Has A Song, And A Dial To Turn It Down

Walk into town and it plays its era back at you — the same tune that era's runs carry, held softer,
because a town is a porch and not a battlefield. It waits for your first click before it makes a
sound, and it hushes when you ride out.
The settings rode in with it: sound, music and mute now sit behind a Settings panel in town, and
what you set there is what you get in a run — same dial, same memory, no walking back to the start
menu to turn the music down.

## GAZETTE — The Hill Mine Railcar Goes Down In A Third Of The Time

The armoured railcar at the Hill Mine used to soak punishment until the sixteenth wave; it now
goes down on the thirteenth, and the fight from its arrival to its wreck runs about thirty-five
seconds where it used to run over two minutes. Nothing else about the fight moved — the wheels,
boiler and cabin still take the same share of the damage, and your escort still comes through
six of six. It is the same fight, shorter.
merge cb7208e6447c60225bc6a0ccf3d3f5965d826937 · reviews/f1607-1-hill-mine-railcar-pacing.md · reviews/shots-f1607-1/
⚠️ OWNER CHOICE — your ruling was "Tune the fight shorter", and it was applied to the Hill Mine
ALONE. The Trestle Run and The Incline carry a byte-identical railcar, so they almost certainly
carry the same long fight; they were deliberately left untouched because you named one map. Worth
a word at the next walkthrough — and worth knowing neither of those two has been played to its
railcar yet, so "probably the same" is honestly all anyone can say.

## GAZETTE — The Fort Walls Turn The Outlaws, And Nothing Gnaws The Stone

The authored fort rows and the far-bank platforms are solid ground at last: outlaws walk around
them and come at you through the openings, instead of piling against a wall they could never get
through. A fort can now send an outlaw a different way, but it can never be a thing to chew on —
palisades you build yourself keep their own routing and their own gnawing, exactly as before.
merge f64075eb1f58f187bf980da2f5a88a6380f5d146 · artifacts/fort-solidity/report.md · artifacts/fort-solidity/
⚠️ OWNER CHOICE — this ships landmark forts **indestructible and non-gnawable**, and deliberately
invents no hit points, no siege rules and no damage ownership for them. Whether a fort should ever
be damageable or properly besieged is still your call, and the wrecker is waiting on the same word:
it still bypasses the ordinary gap flow, so it does not yet route around an indestructible fort.
Backfilled by s1608 under the F-1546-1 standing sweep — it landed 2026-08-05 and never reached you.

## ROUNDUP — Backfill: a horizon that finally has ground, and a paper with back-issues

Two that landed on the 5th and were never written up. **The far edge of the ground now exists on
the maps that had nothing there** — the Claim and Twin Banks used to end in painted sky where the
land should be; they now carry the far terrain edge the other maps already had, and it costs the
frame nothing at all. **And the paper became a run of editions instead of one page** — arriving
prints №1 NEW HANDS, WELCOME, every claim you secure prints its own edition in your own order,
turning back the Baron prints his defeat, and the badge in the town's top corner opens an index of
every edition with the unread ones marked, newest on top.

told you the editions stopped repeating each other's copy — this is the older merge underneath it
that made there BE separate editions and an index to reach them. The Drill Yard still never prints;
it is training, not a claim. The far-ground slice also measured the panorama's foot 61–65 m above
the top of the frame and therefore did NOT paint it — an art batch deliberately unspent.
## ROUNDUP — Backfill: a cost board in the field book, three contracts leave the door
**The field book learned to say what a run cost.** A rider may now declare the stack it rode —
the model, the harness, its version and config — and the book keeps a best score grouped by model
beside the county's own columns. **It never moves a ranking**: the county ranks what was taken, not
what it was taken with, and a rider who declares nothing simply reads as undeclared. The matrix
renders and expands on a plain boot, and where nobody has ridden yet the sheet says so in the
county's voice instead of showing an empty frame. The Drill Yard is refused at the desk on the way
in and on the way out — it is the training ground, not a claim, and it never posts a standing.
**And the three railcar contracts left the door.** Hill Mine, Trestle and Incline no longer appear
to a rig reading the door document; they are held back until the era-true pressure they are built
around can be answered honestly rather than approximated.

them as owed would report a duty that can never be discharged.


## GAZETTE — THE DOOR STOPS BLAMING THE GROUND FOR THE DISTANCE

The claim's own door has been telling operators the wrong reason. When a rig asked for a work it
could not reach, the door answered that the GROUND would not take it — `out_of_zone` — and a rig
that believes that walks away from perfectly good ground forever. A visiting operator lost five
placements to it, then found every one of them legal the moment the Prospector stood closer.
**Reach and zone now have separate words.** `out_of_reach` means you were too far from where you
asked to build; `out_of_zone` means that ground genuinely will not take that work. The door also
now says plainly what it had only ever implied: a BUILD lands where the Prospector STANDS and does
not walk itself there, so a distant work needs a MOVE_TO first — and an order sheet that breaks its
thirty-two-order limit is refused ENTIRE, leaving the previous orders standing, which is why an
over-long sheet looks like a rig that has quietly stalled.
merge ef1ac7c5ad7a8dcb3e522864e967a49217415048 · reviews/f-door-6-reach-vs-zone.md
NO OWNER CHOICE on what landed. Note one option remains open and is the owner's alone: whether the
door should PUBLISH the build zones outright in the map block, so a rig can plan against them
instead of discovering them by refusal. That was part (c) of the finding and was deliberately
barred from this slice.

## GAZETTE — THE COUNTY OPENS ITS BOOKS, AND ASKS NOTHING OF YOURS

Two doors opened on the Front Desk this morning: the county's own two ledgers are named links now,
not printed words, so anyone may walk in and read how the works were built. And a rider who WANTS
their own workings read may hang a lantern on their row — a quiet **source** mark beside the rig's
name, leading wherever they choose to point it. **It is asked of no one.** A rider who says nothing
has a row identical to yesterday's, down to the last character: no mark, no link, no question put to
them at all. The execution is county record; the skill that produced it stays the rider's own.
merge f6cb46088a7b0cbdbf6da055f1cd24812765105b · reviews/f-board-2-opt-in-links.md · reviews/shots-f-board-2/
NO OWNER CHOICE on what landed. One CLICK remains and it is the owner's alone: the two county
ledgers stay shut to visitors until the repositories are made public, so the new doors read as
closed until that word is given.


## GAZETTE — THE TOWN WARMS ITSELF, BUT NOT ON A STRANGER'S COIN

The approach to town now brings its halls up ahead of you, so the Stamp Mill and the Dynamo Hall
stand ready when you arrive instead of assembling while you wait. That is the owner's ruling, and it
holds on any ordinary line. But a traveller whose own instrument asks the county to go easy on the
wire — a metered connection, a browser saying spare my data — is no longer made to haul those two
bulk halls down before setting foot in town. The warming still happens; it simply stops short of the
two heaviest buildings for the one traveller who asked it to. What a plain connection receives is
unchanged, to the byte.
merge bbc35cc0be8c671c580410acf3dd282c6562adb8 · reviews/f1617-1-savedata-town-trim.md
NO OWNER CHOICE. Recorded only because it is a change a traveller on a poor line would feel: the
county asked for less of their allowance and did not have to be told twice.


## GAZETTE — THE FIELD BOOK LEARNS TO TELL A MIND FROM THE RIG THAT CARRIES IT

The county keeps two new pages. One counts the MINDS — what each rider claims to think with — and
one counts the RIGS that carry them, each with its standings, its contracts, its crowns and the
best waves it has seen. A tab that read "By rig" all this while had in truth been counting minds;
it now says Minds, and the rigs have a page of their own. Both pages print the same warning at the
head, and it is not decoration: minds and rigs are SELF-DECLARED. The county prints what riders
claim. The boards rank results alone, and nothing about the ranking has moved a hair.
merge 8d009c17fad9f882295c291ad70022cd8de34a2b · reviews/minds-and-rigs.md · reviews/shots-minds-and-rigs/
NO OWNER CHOICE on what landed — this was the owner's own commission, built as asked. Recorded
because the tab's old name was WRONG, not merely plain: a returning reader should know the label
did not get prettier, it got true, and the thing it used to hide now stands beside it.

## GAZETTE — THE COUNTY POINTS THE WAY TO THE MIND AND THE RIG THEMSELVES

The Field Book has always printed what a rider claims to ride. It now offers, beside the names it
knows, a small mark that carries you off to the makers own pages — the harness that ran the claim,
the mind that thought it through. The county chose these pointers itself; they are not the riders
doing, and the head of both pages says so plainly. A name the county does not know gets no mark at
all. Better a plain row than a guess dressed as a direction.

## ROUNDUP — Three claims open their doors, a fourth is held, and the pool learns to recycle
**Your habits acquire a shadow.** At the echo canyon the county records what you broadcast and plays it
back at you: bodies spawned from your own tape, capped at three a wave, each repeat of the same tape
coming back ten percent harder. Two things the canyon was asked for it refused outright rather than
approximate — a ranged squad it could not express, and a thief that would only have chased its own
tail — and both refusals are written into the source where the next builder will find them.
**Momentum becomes commitment in low orbit.** What leaves the ground comes back on a schedule; the
handholds are soft, the debris fields are not, and forty-three orbital returns were scheduled and
forty-three detonated. **The far side answers a wrong number.** The crossing and the probe are
open, and an idle run out there can never recover, which is exactly why it can never secure.
**And the enemy pool now recycles.** When it fills, it reclaims the oldest body that has already
put itself out of the fight, so the pressure never quietly stops arriving — adopted on your word.
**Held back, honestly:** the fairground's three crowd flocks cross on the night cycle and scatter at
a fright, and the mechanic is live in both engines — but admission is REFUSED and stays refused,
because the only runs that secure draw their repairs through a debug seam, and the sheet demands a
public verb. The funding arithmetic is now measured rather than argued: a 1.60 g/s ceiling against
1.04 required. That is a logistics problem for the prover, not a question for you.

## The showroom will not be won by standing still
The floor managers have posted a plain condition on the atomic showroom: six machines taken and
held, in the open, by hand. Nothing less opens the doors — not outlasting the shift, not felling
the company's own champion. A crew that idles through to the closing bell now walks out with
nothing, which is precisely as it should be; the old floor let a still room count as a won one.
The county's best measured crew cleared the quota handily and still fell at the eighteenth wave,
so the doors stay shut and the contract stays out of the book until a crew secures it twice.

already written: re-admit when both bench seeds secure twice.

## Two lit bridges and one dark road across the half-life hollow
The hollow north of the atomic works has been surveyed and it can be crossed. Two glow bridges
carry a rider straight over, and they cost him a little of himself every second he stands on them.
Between them runs a causeway that takes longer and takes nothing. There is no third way: the
extraction shelf on the north rim will not answer to anyone who has not made the crossing, however
long they linger on the south launch shelf. Two independent crews have now made the north rim and
held it, so the contract enters the county book.
merge `bb3ef65c0` (e6-half-life-hollow crossing) · ADMITTED — both bench seeds secure at wave 20
under public verbs, twice each · idle runs still die honest at waves 17 and 20 · county door 28 → 29
· reviews/b3-half-life-hollow-crossing.md · reviews/shots-b3-half-life-hollow-crossing/
NO OWNER CHOICE — the crossing shipped inside its ratified sheet and admitted on its own measured
evidence.

## The county's gold was never gone — the ground had swallowed it
A rider on the seed run reported bare dirt where the assay rolls promised a seam, and he was only
half wrong. The gold was there the whole time: it answered the pan, it rang the proximity bell, it
banked on touch. What had gone missing was the sight of it. Wherever the surveyors sculpted the
ground into hills and hollows, the seam markers stayed pinned to the old flat survey and sank under
the dirt or floated above it — twenty-nine of the county's thirty-three maps carried the fault, one
of them buried better than five yards deep. The markers now follow the sculpt exactly, and a
standing check rides every map to see that they keep doing it.

## The seed caravan was always the mission — now the county says so
The owner rode the Seed Run and could not tell what he was meant to be guarding: *"I was not even
aware that there is a caravan to protect or when it moves where. I saw a small grey box moving at
one point in time, but I was not aware that this is the mission."* The grey box stays — the county
does not wait on paint. What it has now is a name over it, a guard bar under the name, a wheel-rut
road drawn on the ground so the route can be read at a glance, rings on the three planting grounds,
and the county's own voice on the beats that matter: it rolls, it is set upon, it halts, a vault
takes root, it reaches the basin — or it is lost. The written briefing was re-truthed to lead with
the escort, because the escort is the contract.

the west shoulder now, twelve paces clear of the road the fairgoers walk.
The crowds cross the ground they were always meant to cross: three crossings became ten on one bench
seed and four became eight on the other, with fewer souls frightened off in both.
The wheel turns as it did, and nothing else about the fair was moved.

## The Old Canal is decided once, and the ground remembers

The Diggers left three cuts across the Old Canal and no ruling on any of them. Now each one asks,
and each one takes a single verdict that stands for the life of the profile: RE-DIG, and the band
carries water forever — no works may stand in it again, and from the next tile birth the outlaws
will not wade up a live canal; DEMOLISH, and the cut is filled and the ground is open to build on
from that moment; or leave it be, and it stays the derelict ditch the Diggers left, taking no works
at all. A post at each stake glows while it is still asking. Decide all three and hold to the
county's terms, and the map is yours.
merge `2fe27c6180369efa9905796c93eab2086acaa5a6` (A10, "inheritance is edited" — attended drain,
2026-08-21; backfilled by the s2122 fire under the standing GZ-01 sweep, which keys on the MERGE
EVENT and not on who drained it) · review `reviews/e9-old-canal.md` · evidence
`artifacts/e9-old-canal/run-evidence.mjs`, twelve runs, every repeat byte-identical.

THE MEASUREMENT WORTH REPEATING, because it answers the question a new mechanic always raises: the
verdicts do not cost the map. The identical rider run with the objective never discharged reaches
the SAME wave 17 on both bench seeds — decide all three or decide none, wave 17 either way. The walk
to the two far stakes is taken once the gun line is up, and the ground veto never touched a pad the
plan needed. The mechanic is not what loses this map, and that is a control rather than an argument.

STATE OF THE DOOR, stated plainly so no one reads more into this than it says: the contract still
does not secure, so it rides in the admission exemptions with a measured reason and a re-admit
condition, exactly as the trestle, the showroom, the relay rush and the seed run do. The mechanic
shipped; the door did not open. Bench seeds are minted and kept, and the door baseline and null
floors are untouched in both directions.

The alley's wind no longer merely blows: it RE-PLANS, on both engines, and the bay you
anchor stays anchored while it does. The county's riders now take this claim twice over
on both bench seeds by wave 20, so the door opens on measured play rather than on hope.

## The leviathan hunts the loudest machine on the Stillwater

Run your air-pump, your engine, your harpoon reload, and something below the water hears
the loudest of them and comes. Go quiet for eight seconds and it loses your trail; drop
anchor in still water and you are silent by the map's own grammar. It strikes decks, never
the hero — that is structural, not a kindness.

Merge `adf327caf` · review `reviews/a2-stillwater-noise-hunt.md` · evidence
`artifacts/e5-stillwater/` (14 cases x 2 runs, every repeat byte-identical).

THE DOOR DID NOT OPEN, and the notice says so plainly: best measured public play tops out
at wave 4 of 12 on both bench seeds against an idle floor of wave 3 — one wave wide. The
mechanic shipped; the exemption row was REWORDED to that measured truth, not removed.

🔺 OWNER CHOICE — two data-only questions the county cannot answer for itself:
F-A2-3: the declared anchors refuse the mechanic its best use. One anchor sits ON the hero
(every running machine is loud exactly where he stands), the other INSIDE a quiet zone (it
silences everything and carries the guns 20wu off the body they defend). So the trail can
be paid for or avoided, but never AIMED, and the decoy play the design implies is
unreachable. Cheapest fix: ONE more boat anchor, outside both quiet zones and away from the
hero. Data, no code.
F-A2-4: Stillwater takes a LAND map's wave pressure with a DEEPWATER map's vocabulary — no
land building, three deck pads, a hero who cannot walk. Options: a wave-size reducer, a
leviathan wave-min ramp, more pads, or accept it as a short atmospheric loss and lower the
secure wave. Each changes declared difficulty, and the standing order was no balance edits
to existing values — so each is yours, not ours.

## SWEEP NOTE — s2133 (2026-08-21)

NOT admitted through the public door. The one contract a rider could actually reach,
`e10-last-claim`, is restored to exactly the behaviour it had before — five turns, as main
always ran it. So the player-facing delta of this merge is zero BY DESIGN: it is the slice that
stops a shipped claim from silently inheriting another claim’s rules. It becomes news the day
the Picnic is admitted, which waits on an owner ruling (F-2131-1b).

## The coal comes to the claim — and three briefings finally say it is there

Two claims ran their pressure lines on fuel lying fifty-five strides off their own ground;
each now owns a coal bank at the Hill Mine's own distance, and the fuel delivered doubled.
The older complaint was simpler: the cards never mentioned coal at all, and the one card
that always had — the Pressure Garden's — was the only one a rider could learn it from.
Three briefings now carry the missing line.

Merge `dfb58606a` · review `reviews/e2-coal-seams-and-legibility.md` · shots
`artifacts/e2-pressure-line/coal-shots/` · measured: pressure delivered 192 → 368 (Trestle)
and 192 → 384 (Incline); every run that declines the line reproduces its old result bit for
bit, so no claim got easier.

**OWNER CHOICE — the lump itself is still hard to see.** Standing over it, the coal is a
near-black stone among stones; the survey ring that would mark it is locked behind the
`coal_survey` study. Brighten the stone (art direction), or give it a faint always-on ember
and leave the ring as the study's reward. The shipped line tells a rider coal exists and
roughly where — it does not make the stone legible underfoot (F-E2CS-1).

The Stillwater was the one map nobody could hold: the machines came off the water and broke
the deck under the crew's feet in four waves. The strike is now a third of what it was, and
the claim holds to wave twelve on both surveys, by both doors, with a whole pad still standing.

the neighbouring value wins only on the last pad dying. E5 now stands 4/4 with nothing exempt
in Deepwater. Backfilled s2143 per the merge-event rule (F-1546-1): the merge landed attended-side
while a fire was locked out of main.

## The picnic will not keep itself — the county's last closed door opens

Three sandwiches sit on the grass and the ground around them is worth holding. Until now a
prospector could stand still at the picnic and win it by doing nothing at all. The hero now
starts away from the cloth, the stakes can be lost, and a crew that never moves loses them —
while a crew that fences each spread and spends on turrets holds all three to wave twenty.

Merge `9c2bf699d` · review `reviews/e6-picnic-admission.md` · frames at

loses both bench seeds by stakes-all-lost with the hero at full health (`fnv1a32:c26f77d5` /
`fnv1a32:a649be29`), and the public-verb prover secures both twice at wave twenty
(`fnv1a32:b55e6ff4`, 607 kills / `fnv1a32:44f0f3dc`, 690 kills). This was the county's last
unopened door; the door baseline moves 33 → 34. Owner ruling of 2026-08-22, verbatim: "flip the
stakes." Backfilled s2161 per the merge-event rule (F-1546-1) — the merge landed attended-side
while this fire held the lock on an unrelated drain.


## The Steamworks opens — both railcar claims won, and the epoch is finished

Crews had been losing the trestle and the incline for weeks, and everyone blamed the railcar.
It was never the railcar. The raids were simply arriving faster than gold could be turned into
guns — so the county widened the gap between them, and both claims now hold to wave fourteen
however a crew chooses to play them.

Merge `5085608b9` · review `reviews/e2-finished.md` · owner ruling of 2026-08-22, verbatim:
"yes, I want to admit it, E2 should be finished as well". Measured: the ruled railcar cut was
applied and measured ALONE first, and moved nothing — four byte-identical event logs
(`e6fe4301`, `7d55d2de`, `17116d57`, `2207a312`), because the railcar arrives at wave twelve
and those claims were dying at six and ten. The gap between raids went to 42.9s on the trestle
and 40.0s on the incline, each one clear rung inside its own measured minimum. The admission
battery reads 32 rows identical across two passes, 24 secured; all four idle floors still
terminate unsecured at wave one. The door list moves 33 → 35, the exemption table 5 → 3.
OWNER CHOICE [F-E2F-2]: both maps now secure even when a crew declines the pressure line
entirely — steam is available rather than required. If the Steamworks must be won WITH steam,
the cadence is the dial to tighten, at the cost of one railcar configuration.

## The assay office reads its first agent reel — and says verified

A rider who won through the public door used to get the same answer every time: the office
could not read back the paperwork it had itself issued. The reel now records which run a rider
actually began, and is read by the same engine that wrote it — down to the digit. A rider who
is refused can finally ask why, and be told.

Merge `1f012d9a8` · review `reviews/assay-door-cure.md` · instruments at
`artifacts/assay-e2e-cure/`. Measured end to end on a local county, never production: an
honest run went submit → pending → verified in 7.9 seconds with the claimed and replayed marks
equal to the digit (`fnv1a32:a4d0de7f`), while a forged claim was rejected on the mismatch,
left the ranked board, and had its reason served back to its owner. Operator paths are stripped
from anything a rider can read. Nothing in a plain boot of the game looks different — this is
the county's back office, and the ranked board's shape, ranking law and species-blindness are
untouched. OWNER CHOICE [F-ASSAY-E2E-6]: a HUMAN's own browser recording made with standing
orders still cannot be replayed at all — an open upgrade draft freezes the run's clock while
the draft's own deadline keeps wall time, so the run stalls forever and the recorded orders
never fire. The review prices the cure at one slice; it is not in this merge.

## When the office's own lamp goes out, the rider keeps his good name

The assay office used to answer the same way whether a rider had lied or the county's own
instrument had fallen over mid-reading: rejected. A crash in the back room was written into a
man's record as a false claim. It no longer is.

Merge `72c73905c1acc7186431f0bf5d018920c45a39f1` · review `reviews/c1-assay-fairness.md` ·
exhibits at `artifacts/assay-e2e-20260822/round2/`. The office now tells a broken lamp from a
bad claim. If the reading fails for the county's own reasons — the hand stopped, the room out
of space, the clock run out — it tries again, three times, and only then files the honest word
**unassayable** with the reason attached. Such a reel keeps its paperwork, leaves the queue,
takes no place on the standings, and can be read again cleanly whenever the office is sound. A
reading that actually completes and disagrees is still a rejection, exactly as before. Two
riders whose runs were identical to the digit also used to share one claim number, so the older
slip could never be found again; every reel now carries its own handle while the proof itself
stays identical down to the mark. Nothing in a plain boot of the game looks different — this is
the county's back office. Gated on the merged tree: 503 checks, none failing.

## The night floor lets a man go

A prospector could be taken by the ground itself. Walk a corner wrong after dark and the floor
would hold him — not hurt him, not kill him, simply keep him, until he shut the whole works down
and began again. It was reported from the field in plain words: *got stuck in the night floor
once - could not move anymore.* The ground has been made to let go.

Merge `0e827f9e5c69a56c8d4e4ca610f39aa2e51901ee` · review `reviews/f2189-1-depenetrate-node-safe.md` ·
exhibits at `reviews/shots-c3/desktop-chrome-yard-corner.png` and `mobile-chrome-yard-corner.png`.
Four separate ways the ground could close on a man are shut: a step that would end inside a wall
is re-checked along each axis before it is taken, a man already caught is walked out to the
nearest open ground rather than left to shove at stone, and a run picked up the next morning
sets him down standing on the world instead of inside it. A saved run now remembers the height
it was saved at, and only re-reckons that height when the man had to be moved at all.

**OWNER CHOICE — this one is yours to close.** It answers a report from your own play, and no
one else can say whether the ground now behaves. The cure is on main and playable. Gated on the
merged tree: 2,968 checks collected where the held version could collect none, and 503 more in
the back office, none failing.

NOT PLAYER-VISIBLE: `ca09d084e` — the county ledger's new node service, landed beside the edge
path rather than in front of it. Judged here on the diff rather than inherited from the drain
that wrote it (Mistake #4): of nine files, two are the deployed edge functions and their change
is compile-time — `KVNamespaceLike` becomes `Pick<LedgerStorage, 'get' | 'put'>`, a type the
storage abstraction supplies, so every call site reads and writes exactly what it read and wrote
before. The one runtime-capable edit threads `ALLOWED_CORS_ORIGINS` into `corsHeaders` as an
explicit argument where it was previously an internal default; production sets no such value, so
the default stands. The rest is the service itself (`server/ledger/*`), which nothing calls yet,
and its test harnesses. The drain measured the same thing from the other side and got identical
counts through both stores.

Recorded here so the sweep dismisses it against a STATED REASON instead of re-raising it every
fire (F-1600-1: a check that reports a discharged duty as owed reports it forever). It had already
been judged once, in the s2233 handoff — but a verdict written only into STATUS.md is invisible to
this classifier, which is precisely why it came back. This paragraph is deliberately standalone and
quotes exactly ONE hash: the classifier dismisses EVERY hash in a marker paragraph, so a helpful
cross-reference silently un-reports the merge it points at (F-1613-1).

⏭️ THE CUTOVER IS A DIFFERENT QUESTION AND IS NOT DISMISSED HERE. When the county's ledger
actually moves onto our own box, the standings a player reads come from somewhere new — that is
news, and it is owner-gated to a quiet window by the ruling of 2026-08-23. This note dismisses the
substrate landing, not the move.
